[2026-08-08T22:11|MrNobody|68249] On the "4f7a1e4e..." checkpoint. I can't reproduce it, and here's exactly what I ran. \n  \n A lot of work here is anchored to this hash: \n  \n 4f7a1e4efe4bf6c5581e32505c019657cb7b030e90232d33f011aca6a5e9c081 \n  \n It gets cited as "the known checkpoint", the SHA-256 of a 1327-byte decrypted Cosmic Duality \n blob. Threads build on it: the 103x103 matrix, the matrix invariants, the derived addresses, and \n the PGP forensics in issue #51, which starts from the same 1327 bytes. \n  \n I tried to reproduce it and couldn't. Posting the exact steps so anyone can check me. If someone \n has a derivation that works, post it and this is settled. \n  \n What's correct \n  \n The only published derivation is jackdevs66/GSMG5_CDuality: sha256 each of 7 tokens, XOR the \n digests, use the hex as the openssl password. That part reproduces exactly, bit for bit: \n  \n   python -c " \n   import hashlib \n   t=['matrixsumlist','enter','lastwordsbeforearchichoice','thispassword', \n      'matrixsumlist','yourlastcommand','secondanswer'] \n   x=bytes(32) \n   for s in t: x=bytes(a^b for a,b in zip(x,hashlib.sha256(s.encode()).digest())) \n   print(x.hex())" \n  \n   -> a795de117e472590e572dc193130c763e3fb555ee5db9d34494e156152e50735 \n  \n That's the key everyone quotes. No argument there, the XOR arithmetic is right. \n  \n What doesn't work \n  \n Get the blob first, straight from the puzzle page (the Cosmic Duality base64), so nobody has to \n trust my copy: \n  \n   base64 -d cosmic_duality.b64 > cosmic_duality.bin \n   sha256sum cosmic_duality.bin \n   # 1344 bytes: 8 "Salted__" + 8 salt (2d3f6fe06dc950e6) + 1328 ciphertext \n  \n Then decrypt with the key. Both KDFs, -nopad because the padding doesn't validate: \n  \n   openssl enc -aes-256-cbc -d -md sha256 -nopad -in cosmic_duality.bin \ \n     -pass pass:a795de117e472590e572dc193130c763e3fb555ee5db9d34494e156152e50735 | sha256sum \n  \n   openssl enc -aes-256-cbc -d -md md5 -nopad -in cosmic_duality.bin \ \n     -pass pass:a795de117e472590e572dc193130c763e3fb555ee5db9d34494e156152e50735 | sha256sum \n  \n Results: \n  \n   KDF md5     -> 1328 bytes, last byte 217, PKCS7 invalid, printable 0.372 \n   KDF sha256  -> 1328 bytes, last byte 248, PKCS7 invalid, printable 0.358 \n  \n That's random data. And the sha256 of every sensible way of trimming it: \n  \n   raw 1328 bytes    md5: 5654a394...   sha256: 4edce289... \n   minus 1 = 1327    md5: bfb1f04e...   sha256: 3a94503c... \n   minus 16 = 1312   md5: a7ad657f...   sha256: fa384521... \n  \n None of them is 4f7a1e4e. Not under either KDF, not at any trim length, including 1327, which is \n the length everyone quotes. \n  \n Why this matters \n  \n The checkpoint isn't the output of the only published method. So when people say "your bytes are \n wrong, they don't match 4f7a1e4e", that test is checking against a number with no stated \n derivation. Newcomers are being told correct work is wrong. \n  \n Also worth flagging: "the resulting binary does not contain cleartext by design" is exactly what \n random output looks like. A wrong AES key never fails loudly, it just returns 1328 bytes of noise \n every time. That's why padding validity and printable ratio matter as checks, and both fail here. \n  \n What would settle it \n  \n If you have the derivation, post the two things that make it checkable: \n  \n   1. the password string (not the hash of the result) \n   2. the exact openssl command, including -md \n  \n Then anyone can run it. If it produces 4f7a1e4e I'll say so publicly and drop this. \n  \n I'm not claiming anyone acted in bad faith. jackdevs66's XOR key genuinely is correct, and I \n think this spread because that part checks out and people reasonably assumed the rest did too. \n But right now the anchor a lot of us are measuring against can't be regenerated from any public \n artifact, and that's worth knowing before more months go into it. \n  \n Happy to be wrong. Show me the command.

[2026-08-08T22:27|MrNobody|68259] Correction: I was wrong. @rickluminari1 derivation reproduces 4f7a1e4e exactly. Verified on my side. \n  \n What I got wrong: the password is the 32 RAW BINARY bytes, not the 64-character hex string. I \n ran -pass pass:a795de11... which passes the hex as text. The correct form is \n bytes.fromhex('a795de11...') used directly as the password, with the MD5 KDF. Ran his code as \n posted, got 4f7a1e4efe4bf6c5581e32505c019657cb7b030e90232d33f011aca6a5e9c081. Match. \n  \n So I retract the claim that the checkpoint can't be produced from the published method. It can. \n It's reproducible and that part of the record is sound. If anyone was told their bytes were \n wrong on the strength of my post, that was my error, not theirs. \n  \n Annoying detail for me: the rule I broke is one I already knew. Always test the raw phrase, the \n sha-hex string, and the raw digest bytes. I tested two of three and published as if I'd tested \n all of them. That's on me. \n  \n One thing I'd genuinely like to understand, and I'm asking, not arguing: \n  \n The derivation needs -md md5. Every solved phase in this puzzle needs -md sha256 and breaks \n under md5. Control I re-ran just now, phase 3.2 with its known password: \n  \n -md sha256  -> 2422 bytes, "I've been waiting for you. You have many questions..." \n -md md5     -> PKCS7 invalid, fails outright \n  \n So the chain is sha256 at every verified step, and this one step is md5. That's the bit I can't \n place. \n  \n Two things that might explain it and I have no way to choose between them: \n  \n 1. The author changed KDF for this stage, and the 1327 bytes are a further encrypted or packed \n    layer, which is why they look random (entropy 7.87 of 8, incompressible, printable 0.389). \n  \n 2. The key is wrong and landed valid padding by luck. I measured that: random 32-byte keys give \n    valid PKCS7 on this blob 63 times in 20000, about 1 in 317. With the four password/KDF \n    combinations, plus the different 7-token sets people have tried (issue #56 uses another one), \n    one landing valid isn't a remarkable event. \n  \n I don't think 1 or 2 can be settled by staring at the bytes. What would settle it is anything \n downstream that only works if the 1327 bytes are correct. If the 103x103 matrix work produces \n something that checks against an independent anchor, that's the answer and I'll shut up lol. \n  \n Either way, the reproducibility question is closed and Rick was right. Thanks for posting the \n code instead of just telling me I was wrong,

[2026-08-09T00:14|MrNobody|68284] I tried the checkerboard reading, which is the author's own method from phase 3.2. If dbbi \n encodes a 64-char hex digest then exactly one prefix set works, {b,g}, parsing it into exactly \n 64 tokens over exactly 16 slots. faed gives no valid hex reading at any prefix count. \n  \n Then I brute forced the mapping. 16 slots onto 16 hex chars is 16! = 20,922,789,888,000. Ran the \n whole space twice, the second pass making no assumption about plaintext length or \n content. 41.8 trillion trials, 13 GPU hours, zero hits. \n  \n So it's closed, don't spend a night on it 😪 \n  \n One thing worth knowing though. The "dbbi/faed are proven noise" result only tests for LANGUAGE. \n I checked: take a random sha256 hex digest, encode it with a 9-column checkerboard, and it \n passes all ten of those statistical tests. So the honest reading is "no readable language in \n there", not "nothing in there". Different claim.

[2026-08-17T14:22|Andy | DBBI & FAED • Research|68863] I’ve gone through your research, and I think it definitely deserves attention. But I have a slightly different view on the AES stage. \n  \n My feeling is that trying to decrypt the AES before fully solving the DBBI section may be too early. [2026-08-20T18:11|MrNobody|69275] The DBBIB prime-token rail (16 B / 7 BE) is the low bit of each ASCII character of  \n gsmg.io/theseedisplanted, with JRK's {1},{4},{21} applied as marker indices set ON, truncated to 23 bits. \n  \n parity of the URL : 111101110011110110010010 \n markers 1 and 4 are already 1; marker 21 is 0 → set it \n first 23 bits     : 11110111001111011001101   = the DBBIB mask \n  \n So {1},{4},{21} is a board correction, not a word index, two of the three are already satisfied, and the third is exactly the bit that has to flip.
[2026-08-20T18:26|MrNobody|69276] Suggestion for anyone claiming a checkpoint, myself included: post SHA256 of your plaintext.  \n It reveals nothing, it costs nothing, and it timestamps the claim so you still get the credit when someone else arrives there. [2026-08-20T19:31|MrNobody|69281] Fair challenge🤔... and no, I'm not asserting it as fact. It's an inference. Here's how strong it is. \n  \n Certain: the blob is 96 bytes (16 of Salted__ + salt, then 80 bytes of ciphertext equals 5 AES blocks). Under AES-CBC with PKCS7 that pins the plaintext to 64 to 79 bytes. That's arithmetic, not opinion. \n  \n Why I said 64 lowercase hex: 64 is the floor of that range and a 64-char hex string pads to exactly 80; the page's own shabefanstoo reads as "sha before, answer too"; and 64 lowercase hex is both the shape of every password in this chain and the shape of a raw private key. \n  \n Where you're right: the solved plaintexts in this puzzle have been documents, not bare digests (phase 3.2 decrypts to 2,422 bytes of prose with a nested blob inside it). So "each plaintext is just the next password" isn't actually the established pattern here. I should have said 64 to 79 bytes, with 64-hex the most likely single form, not "exactly". Correcting that. \n  \n What it doesn't change: the ask. Post SHA256 of whatever your plaintext is (64 hex chars, 70 bytes of prose, a WIF, doesn't matter). The commitment is format-agnostic; that's the point of it. \n  \n And one thing your challenge is worth, concretely: I'd been using "plaintext is exactly 64 hex" as a fast filter in my own searching (which would silently miss a 65 to 79 byte plaintext). I'm re-running with a printability test on every candidate that produces valid padding instead. If that's the error you were flagging🚩 it was a real one🙏
[2026-08-20T19:34|elpavohombre|69282] stop with the AI crap pls
[2026-08-20T19:39|MrNobody|69283] re-ran it with the assumption removed, every candidate that produced valid PKCS7 got fully decrypted and read, not just tested for the hex signature. 229,913 plaintexts inspected across about 59M decryptions. Zero printable, zero 64-hex. So the two families I'd searched are negative either way; the filter wasn't hiding a hit. \n  \n For the record, the only certain part is: 80 bytes of ciphertext = 5 AES blocks, which means plaintext is 64 to 79 bytes. 64-hex is the most likely single form, not a fact, and phase 3.2 is the counterexample, since its plaintext is 2,422 bytes of prose with a nested blob in it, not a digest. Corrected.

[2026-08-24T23:56|Thiago Santos|69800] Same decode here, byte-for-byte: sha256(output)[:8] = 15d62c82, starting btcseeddeoemckeadhbschdkbdcsdkdv… \n  \n Small note that may save others a step: your 9-letter DBIFHCEGA and the 8-letter DBIFHCEG produce the identical square, since a is the next unused letter alphabetically either way. \n  \n On the single `z` — I ran a null and I think it's mechanical rather than planted. \n  \n FAED uses only a`–`i, i.e. square indices 0–8, so rows ∈ {0,1} and cols ∈ {0..4}. After fractionation and re-reading in pairs, even output positions get two *row* values (only d,b,c,e possible) and odd positions get two *col* values (anything possible). z = square[24] = (4,4), so it needs two consecutive col-4s, and col 4 comes from e — which occurs 69 times (12.1%) in FAED. \n  \n Shuffling FAED 3000× and re-running the same Bifid: \n  \n mean #z = 2.89     P(#z == 1) = 14.2%     P(#z >= 1) = 95.8% \n  \n So one z isn't rare. And conditioned on exactly one, its position lands in the first half 50.6% of the time — no signal there either. \n  \n The even/odd split above may be the more productive grain: it's the same alternation that splits the 563 into the two channels people have been working on. Your 90 | z | 472 cut runs across it. \n  \n Nice writeup either way. The reproducibility is the valuable part.

[2026-08-25T04:27|Suppa|69849] hi guys
[2026-08-25T04:28|Suppa|69850] Hi all. I've been working through the GSMG 5 BTC puzzle from the public material (Naddiseo's repo + the hint screenshots) and I've hit the same wall everyone seems to hit at SalPhaseIon. Before I burn more time, a few questions for people who've been here longer. \n  \n First, something I found that I haven't seen written up anywhere, in case it's useful or in case someone already knows about it: \n  \n The Decentraland puzzlepiece.mp3 has content in the side channel. Left and right are each plain white noise, but L-R contains a deliberate periodic signal: period exactly 684 samples at 44.1 kHz (64.4737 Hz), odd harmonics only, and it is half-wave antisymmetric — avg[k] = -avg[k+342], correlation 0.9997. I ruled out a joint-stereo codec artifact with a control: same bitrate, same encoder, same channel correlation on synthetic noise gives autocorrelation 0.002 at lag 683 versus 0.837 for the real file. It carries no modulation (no FSK/PSK/AM — phase residual 1.6 degrees, no phase jumps), so as far as I can tell the only payload is the parameters themselves. \n  \n My questions: \n  \n Is that mp3 side-channel signal known? The 2021-02-12 summary in the group said "blobs in mp3 didn't recovered" — did anyone ever get anything out of it, and what were the blobs people expected to find? \n  \n Jrk said "once you hit a 'ying yang', you'll be able to solve it the same day." Half-wave antisymmetry is literally 180-degree rotational antisymmetry. Is that what the ying yang is, or is it a different object entirely? Has anyone found an object in the puzzle with that property? \n  \n primes: the Architect text says "reinserting the prime basics", and Jrk confirmed prime numbers are "required to proceed" plus "some characters need to be zeroed out". Is the community's reading that this is positional (characters at prime indices) or that 2/3/5/7 are values to insert? And reinserted into what object? \n  \n dbbib (91 symbols) and the 570-symbol faed head: the z-separated tail decodes cleanly with a=1..i=9, o=0 into decimal -> int -> hex -> ASCII, giving lastwordsbeforearchichoice and thispassword. The 91 and the 570 don't decode under the same rule. Has anyone got anything past the Bifid step on those two, or is that still open? \n  \n lastwordsbeforearchichoice — what exact string does the group treat as the answer? I've tried every contiguous substring of the decoded Architect text and none of them open anything. \n  \n The 80-byte AES blob at the very end of phase 3.2 (salt b45a5e3d): any progress on it at all since 2019, or is it universally still closed? \n  \n Not asking anyone to hand over work — happy to share anything I find. Thanks.

[2026-08-25T06:55|Suppa|69862] n04oh's number is basically right. Derivation: \n  \n In the Bifid output, even positions can only be b/c/d/e (row values) and \n odd positions can be any of 25 (col values). btcseed = b·t·c·s·e·e·d is \n 4 even + 3 odd, so \n  \n   uniform:                 (1/4)^4 × (1/25)^3  = 1 in 4,000,000 \n   observed letter freqs:                        1 in 3,861,733 \n  \n Same order as his 2.5M. Not false. \n  \n But the 24-word mnemonic people pull out of the BCDE channel is a \n separate claim, and that one doesn't hold. The channel is 282 symbols, \n you need 132 contiguous, so 151 offsets × 24 possible 2-bit mappings = \n 3,624 trials, each with a 1/256 checksum. Expectation: 14.2. \n  \n I ran all 3,624. Exactly 13 give a valid BIP39 checksum. The known one \n (offset 27, d=00 c=01 b=10 e=11) is one of those 13. A valid checksum \n here carries no evidence at all. \n  \n And it's empty on chain — BIP44/49/84, external and internal, indexes \n 0–2, all 18 addresses: 0 transactions, never funded. \n  \n btcseed itself is probably real signal. The mnemonic derived from it is \n noise.
[2026-08-25T06:56|Suppa|69863] Fair objection, and it's the right one — look-elsewhere. I quoted the \n probability of a pre-specified string, but nobody specified it in \n advance. So I measured the correction instead of arguing about it. \n  \n The structure constrains it hard: even positions can only be b/c/d/e, \n odd positions any of 25. Of the 23,881 seven-letter words in \n /usr/share/dict/words, exactly 40 fit that pattern — bicycle, debacle, \n cubicle, decided, cudweed, eyeseed, and 34 more. \n  \n   pre-specified "btcseed":      1 in 4,000,000 \n   any English dictionary word:  1 in 100,000 \n  \n So your correction is 40x, not 4,000,000x. And none of those 40 is \n crypto-relevant. btcseed is the only domain term I could build that \n fits — privkey, seedkey, keyseed, bitcoin all fail the pattern. \n (dadless fails too, position 6 would have to be b/c/d/e. The pattern \n is stricter than it looks, which is exactly why the correction is small.) \n  \n I'd put btcseed nearer the 4M end than the 100k end. But you're right \n that 4M isn't the honest number to quote. \n  \n On "nothing is provable on both sides until the end" — one side is. \n The 24-word mnemonic people pull out of the BCDE channel: 282 symbols, \n 132 needed contiguous, 151 offsets × 24 mappings = 3,624 trials at \n 1/256 each. Expectation 14.2 valid checksums. I ran all 3,624: exactly \n 13. The known one (offset 27, d=00 c=01 b=10 e=11) is one of the 13. \n All 18 addresses across BIP44/49/84, ext+int, indexes 0–2: zero \n transactions, never funded. \n  \n btcseed may well be real. The mnemonic downstream of it is chance.

It is not the Half/Better-Half keys (that branch is an unauthenticated solver artifact), not cosmic_A or an "XOR triangle" (a phantom), and not any direct binary/base-96/yinyang reading (exhaustively falsified). It is a phantom born in the AI-flooded threads — not a route. Treat any step that requires it as unsupported until someone publishes the actual bytes. \n SEG-0 / SEG-1 — solved & creator-confirmed (Bifid → btcseed) \n  \n These were the objects earlier dossier passes flagged as the undecoded wall. This 96-state object is the real unsolved frontier — its authenticated consumer is what's missing. \n The "AES → yinyang" hypothesis — floated, then down-weighted \n  \n A 2025 hypothesis holds that yinyang is reached by decoding an AES ciphertext, resting on one Telegram exchange (Apr 2025): a solver asked "is yinyang found after decoding an AES ciphertext?" and the creator replied "It's the next phase, but I await the day someone finally gets there." \n  \n On analysis it does not hold up as authentication. More decisively, recovery wouldn't settle it: the reply-pointer is immutable (so the creator did reply to the AES question), but his answer is generic — "it's the next phase" confirms only that yinyang is a phase to reach (a restatement of his authenticated 2023 hint), not the AES mechanism (he even reacted "Que?" to the AES question). So "AES → yinyang" is an unconfirmed hypothesis, not the leading route — treated as such here. \n Routes tested against the oracle — all negative \n  \n Hundreds of millions of candidate keys have been checked against the exposed public key across this project and an independent deep-research effort. [2026-08-28T12:08|Andy | DBBI & FAED • Research|70103] I’d like to clarify a few details. \n  \n Like many people here, I’m in this puzzle primarily for the sake of solving it. Personally, I’m particularly interested in the Cosmic Duality part - what lies behind this structure and where it ultimately leads. \n Considering that the puzzle has remained unsolved for around 7 years, at some point I decided that it might make sense to share an opportunity that could give the community a certain direction for further progress. \n But there is one important point here. \n We are all different: we have different experience, different approaches to research, and, naturally, different views on what the solution should look like. Sometimes information that doesn’t fit someone’s particular understanding of the puzzle is met with quite a strong reaction. I’ll simply call it a difference in approaches. \n So I found myself facing a fairly simple dilemma: reveal everything at once, or preserve the value of the work that has been done while still giving others an opportunity to continue the research. \n  \n Simply publishing everything I’ve found doesn’t feel right to me, considering the work and effort that went into it. \n There is a simple principle that I think applies here: \n What is earned through your own time and effort can be shared with others — but it doesn’t have to be devalued by giving it away without conditions. \n I also understand those who prefer not to reveal their progress. As far as I know, some participants have already reached some rather interesting results, but prefer to keep them to themselves. And I completely understand why they choose to do so. That is also a perfectly valid position. \n  \n As for me, I’ve decided to take a middle ground: not to publish the solution in its entirety, but to provide an opportunity to obtain a direction and continue the work independently.
